=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
Takahashi aggregated usage records from ticket gates.
However, he accidentally erased some records of entering and exiting stations.
He is trying to restore the erased records.

You are given a string S consisting of i and o. We want to insert zero or more characters at arbitrary positions in S so that the resulting string satisfies the following conditions:

- Its length is even, and every odd-numbered (1st, 3rd, ...) character is i while every even-numbered (2nd, 4th, ...) character is o.

Find the minimum number of characters that need to be inserted. It can be proved under the constraints of this problem that by inserting an appropriate finite number of characters, S can be made to satisfy the conditions.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
S

Output

Print the answer.

Constraints


- S is a string of length between 1 and 100, consisting of i and o.

Sample Input 1

ioi

Sample Output 1

1

We can insert o after the 3rd character to form ioio to satisfy the conditions. The conditions cannot be satisfied by inserting zero or fewer characters.

Sample Input 2

iioo

Sample Output 2

2

We can insert o after the 1st character and i after the 3rd character to satisfy the conditions. The conditions cannot be satisfied by inserting one or fewer characters.

Sample Input 3

io

Sample Output 3

0

S already satisfies the conditions.

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python

```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

import sys
def main():
    S = sys.stdin.readline().strip()
    t = 0
    for ch in S:
        while ch != ('i' if t % 2 == 0 else 'o'):
            t += 1
        t += 1
    if t % 2 == 1:
        t += 1
    print(t - len(S))
if __name__ == "__main__": main()

Let's think of proof of greedy matching: The process of inserting target chars until current S char matches exactly constructs the lexicographically smallest prefix of target that contains S as subsequence. At each step, if current S char doesn't match next target char, we must insert that target char before it in any valid final string? Is that necessarily? Suppose current S char c doesn't match next target char. Could we instead match c later and insert something else? The greedy algorithm inserts the mismatched target char and keeps c for later. This is standard. But let's prove to ourselves: We are scanning S left to right and target pattern. At any point, t is the length of the prefix of T that has been determined (either by consuming S chars or inserting chars). The next char of T must be pattern[t]. If current S char equals it, we can consume it as that T char. If not, then in any final T, the next T char cannot be matched to current S char; it must be an inserted char (or matched to some later S char? Wait, could the next T char be matched to a later S char while current S char is matched to an even later T char? In subsequence matching, T positions are assigned to S chars in order. The next T position must be assigned to some S char (possibly current or later) or be an inserted char not assigned to any S char. If it's not assigned to current S char, it could be assigned to a later S char, meaning current S char would be matched to a later T position. But then we would have skipped current S char? No, we cannot skip S chars; every S char must be matched to some T char. If next T char is matched to a later S char, then current S char would need to be matched to an even later T char, which is possible? Wait, in a subsequence, the first S char must be matched to some T position. It doesn't have to be the first T position; T can have inserted chars before it. So if current S char doesn't match next T char, we could insert the next T char and still match current S char later. That's what greedy does. But could we instead match the next T char to a later S char and match current S char to a later T char, avoiding inserting the mismatched target char? No, the next T char itself is fixed by pattern; if current S char doesn't equal it, no S char at current position can match it, but a later S char might match it. However, if we match a later S char to this next T position, then the current S char must be matched to a T position after that later S char's position? Wait order: S positions are matched to increasing T positions. If later S char (say S[j], j>current) matches next T position p, then current S char (S[i]) must match some T position < p, because S[i] comes before S[j] in S. But p is the next T position after all previously matched positions; there is no T position < p available except already used. Thus current S[i] cannot be matched after S[j] if S[j] matches p. Therefore, if the next T position is not matched to current S[i], it must be an inserted char not matched to any S char. So greedy insertion is forced. This is a good proof. Thus algorithm constructs minimal T.

Actually careful: The next T position after the prefix matched so far could be matched to current S[i] if it equals pattern[t]. If it doesn't equal, it cannot be matched to current S[i]. Could it be matched to a later S[j]? As argued, then current S[i] would need to be matched to an earlier T position, but all earlier positions are already used. Since T positions are assigned in order to S positions, the first unmatched T position must be assigned to the first unmatched S position if it is assigned to any S position. If it is not assigned to that S position, it must be unassigned (inserted). Because if assigned to a later S position, the first unmatched S position would have no earlier T position left. So yes, forced. Thus greedy is optimal.

```python
import sys

def main():
    S = sys.stdin.readline().strip()
    t = 0
    for ch in S:
        while ch != ('i' if t % 2 == 0 else 'o'):
            t += 1
        t += 1
    if t % 2 == 1:
        t += 1
    print(t - len(S))

import sys
def main():
    S = sys.stdin.readline().strip()
    ans = 0
    t = 0
    for ch in S:
        if ch != ('i' if t % 2 == 0 else 'o'):
            ans += 1
            t += 1
        t += 1
    if t % 2 == 1:
        ans += 1
    print(ans)

Let's prove the simplified algorithm more rigorously:
We maintain `need` = the character that must appear next in the final string if we have already constructed the shortest prefix that can contain the processed part of S. Initially need='i'. For each char c of S:
- If c == need, we can place c as the next final character. Then the next final character must be the opposite of c, so need = opp(c).
- If c != need, then the next final character must be `need`, but c cannot be placed there. We must insert `need` (one insertion). After inserting it, the next final character is opp(need) = c, so c can be placed there. After placing c, the next final character is opp(c) = need (because c=opp(need)). So need remains the same. In both cases, after processing c, need = opp(c). Wait, in mismatch case, need remains same = opp(c), yes. So we can unify update `need = opp(c)` after possibly incrementing ans if c != need.
At the end, if need == 'o', the constructed prefix has odd length; to satisfy even length we append one 'o'. If need == 'i', length is even. This is because the next needed char indicates parity: need='i' at even length (0,2,4...), need='o' at odd length (1,3,5...). So answer = ans + (need == 'o').

Let's test with S="ioi": after processing, need='o', append. Good.
S="io": need='i', no append. Good.
S="i": need='o', append. Good.
S="o": process o mismatch ans1, need='i', no append. Good.

This code is:
```python
import sys

def main():
    S = sys.stdin.readline().strip()
    need = 'i'
    ans = 0
    for ch in S:
        if ch != need:
            ans += 1
        need = 'o' if ch == 'i' else 'i'
    if need == 'o':
        ans += 1
    print(ans)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```
Let's double-check with sample 2 "iioo":

ch i != o ans1, need o (since ch i -> o).
ch o match, need i.
ch o != i ans2, need i.
end need i no append. print2. Good.

Let's decide final code. I think the simplified ans version is best. Let's write:

```python
import sys

def main():
    S = sys.stdin.readline().strip()
    need = 'i'
    ans = 0
    for ch in S:
        if ch != need:
            ans += 1
        need = 'o' if ch == 'i' else 'i'
    if need == 'o':
        ans += 1
    print(ans)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

```python
import sys

def main():
    S = sys.stdin.readline().strip()
    need = 'i'
    ans = 0
    for ch in S:
        if ch != need:
            ans += 1
        need = 'o' if ch == 'i' else 'i'
    if need == 'o':
        ans += 1
    print(ans)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

Let's think if there is any issue with `need = 'o' if ch == 'i' else 'i'` when ch is 'i' and need was 'o' (mismatch): need stays 'o'. When ch is 'o' and need was 'i': need stays 'i'. When match, need flips. Good.

Let's maybe consider if we can use `need = 'i' if need == 'o' else 'o'` after match only, but unified update is correct. Let's prove unified update one more time with a mismatch:
Before processing ch, need is next target char. If ch != need, we insert need. After insertion, the next target char is opposite(need). Since ch != need and only two chars, ch = opposite(need). So ch matches the next target char. After consuming ch, the next target char is opposite(ch) = need. So need after processing is need. The unified update `need = opposite(ch)` gives opposite(ch) = need. Good.
If ch == need, after consuming ch, next target char is opposite(ch). Unified update gives opposite(ch). Good.
So unified update is correct.

S="o" + "io"*50 (length101): first o mismatch ans1 need i; then "io"*50 matches? Let's see after first o, need i. Then ch2 i match need o; ch3 o match need i; ... after 100 remaining chars, need? Since "io" repeated 50 ends with o, after last o need i. End need i no append. ans1. Final length102? S length101 +1 =102. T="ioio...io" length102? S "oioio..." matches positions 2..102? Last char o? Wait S length101: "o" + 50*"io" = 1+100=101, last char o. T length102 has last char o at pos102. S last o matches pos102. Insertions: only initial i. Good. Code ans1. Nice.

Now, let's think if there is any issue with "minimum number of characters that need to be inserted" and our ans maybe counts insertions of chars that could be placed after the entire S but before some S char? No, insertions are placed at arbitrary positions; our construction places them before current S char when needed. This is valid. The final string order is: previously processed S chars, then inserted char(s), then current S char, etc. Good.

Let's consider if there is a possibility to insert a character after current S char instead of before to satisfy a future mismatch, reducing total insertions? The greedy algorithm only inserts when forced before current char. Could inserting after current char help avoid an insertion before a future char? Let's think. Suppose current char matches need, we consume it. Could we instead insert a char after it (which would be the next target char) and then match future S char to it, maybe reducing insertions later? But if we insert a char after current char, that char is not needed to match current char; it would be an extra insertion. It could potentially be matched to a future S char, but then the future S char would not need to be matched to a later target char, perhaps reducing later insertions? Wait, in our construction, every target char is either matched to an S char or inserted. If we insert a char after current char, it is an insertion. Later, if a future S char matches that inserted char, then that future S char would be consumed at that position, and the insertion would not be an "extra" in the sense it is matched to S. But in our construction, we only insert chars when the current S char doesn't match the next target char. Could there be a strategy where we insert a char that is later matched to a future S char, and this avoids a different insertion later, resulting in same or fewer total insertions? Let's examine.

This is a subtle point. Our greedy algorithm constructs a target string T by inserting chars only when necessary to allow the current S char to be matched. But is it always optimal to match the current S char as early as possible? In subsequence matching, matching early is optimal for minimizing the position of the last matched char, but the number of insertions is final length - n. Minimizing final length is equivalent to minimizing last matched position (with parity adjustment). Matching current S char as early as possible minimizes last matched position. But could inserting a char now that is matched to a future S char allow the current S char to be matched later and reduce the final length? Let's test with a concrete example.

Consider S="ioi". Greedy: match i pos1, o pos2, i pos3, append o -> T length4, insertions1. Alternative: insert i at pos1? But T must start i. If we insert i at pos1 and match first S char i to pos3? Let's try T length4 "ioio": S "ioi" can match positions 1,2,3 or 1,2,? Actually greedy uses no insertions before first three, append one. Alternative T length4 same. No extra.
Consider S="oio". Greedy: insert i pos1, match o pos2, i pos3, o pos4 -> T length4, insertions1. Alternative: Could we match first o to pos2, second i to pos3, third o to pos4. That's what greedy does. Need insert i pos1. No alternative with fewer.
Consider S="ooi". Greedy: insert i pos1, match o pos2, insert i pos3, match o pos4, match i pos5, append o pos6 -> T length6, insertions3. Is there a T length4 "ioio" containing "ooi"? We said no. What about T length6 with insertions2? T length6 has 4 inserted? Wait S length3, T length6 => insertions3. To have insertions2, T length5, but final length must be even, so T length4 or6. T length4 no. So 3. Good.
Consider S="iooi". Greedy T length6 insertions2. Could there be T length4 with insertions1? No. Good.
Consider a case where inserting a char before current char might be matched to a future S char instead, allowing current char to match a later target char and reducing total length? Let's formalize: We are looking for minimal even L such that S is subsequence of (io)^L. This is equivalent to finding the earliest position in the infinite pattern where all S chars can be matched in order. The greedy algorithm for subsequence matching to a fixed string (infinite pattern) indeed finds the earliest possible ending position. This is a standard theorem: To find if a sequence is a subsequence of another, the greedy left-to-right matching gives the smallest possible position for each matched element. Therefore no alternative can yield a smaller ending position. Since L is monotonic in ending position, greedy is optimal. So no subtle issue.

Let's prove the standard theorem quickly: Let G_k be the position in T of the k-th matched char under greedy. Let O_k be the position of the k-th matched char under any valid matching. Claim G_k <= O_k for all k. Base k=1: G_1 is the first occurrence of S[1] in T, so <= any occurrence. Inductive: G_{k-1} <= O_{k-1}. Greedy chooses the first occurrence of S[k] after G_{k-1}. O_k is an occurrence of S[k] after O_{k-1}, hence after G_{k-1} (since O_{k-1} >= G_{k-1}). Therefore O_k is one of the occurrences considered by greedy, so G_k <= O_k. Thus greedy minimizes last position. Good. Our algorithm is greedy.

```python
import sys

def main():
    S = sys.stdin.readline().strip()
    need = 'i'
    ans = 0
    for ch in S:
        if ch != need:
            ans += 1
        need = 'o' if ch == 'i' else 'i'
    if need == 'o':
        ans += 1
    print(ans)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

import sys

def main():
    S = sys.stdin.readline().strip()
    need = 'i'
    ans = 0
    for ch in S:
        if ch != need:
            ans += 1
        need = 'o' if ch == 'i' else 'i'
    if need == 'o':
        ans += 1
    print(ans)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Now, let's think if there is any scenario where after processing all S, need='o' but the last target position is odd and the appended char is o, but what if the final string would then have two o's in a row? No, because need='o' means the next char should be o; the previous char was i (since pattern alternates and previous position even). Let's verify S="ii": after processing, target prefix "ioi" (positions i,o,i), previous char i, next need o. Append o -> "ioio". Good. S="oi": after processing, target prefix "ioi" (insert i, o, i), previous i, next o. Append o -> "ioio". Good.

Now, let's think if there is any issue with "minimum number of characters that need to be inserted" and our ans counts insertions before each S char plus append. But what about insertions between two S chars that are not immediately before the second S char? In our construction, all insertions are immediately before the S char that follows them (or at end). This is valid because arbitrary positions. Good.

Let's consider if there is a possible final string where an inserted char is placed between two S chars but not immediately before the second, e.g., S="ab", T="a x y b" with inserted x,y. Our construction would place insertions before b if needed. It doesn't matter; any placement is valid. The count is what matters.

```python
import sys

def main():
    S = sys.stdin.readline().strip()
    need = 'i'
    ans = 0
    for ch in S:
        if ch != need:
            ans += 1
        need = 'o' if ch == 'i' else 'i'
    if need == 'o':
        ans += 1
    print(ans)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Example S="ii": after second i mismatch, need='o', append o. T="ioio"? Let's construct: start need i; ch1 i match -> need o; ch2 i mismatch insert o, consume i -> need o. Target positions: pos1 i (matched S1), pos2 o (inserted), pos3 i (matched S2), pos4 o (appended). T="ioio". Good. If need='o' after a match, e.g., S="ioi": pos1 i, pos2 o, pos3 i, append o. T="ioio". Good. So append always o.

Now, let's think if there is any scenario where after processing all S, need='o' but the last target position is odd and the appended char is o, but what if the final string would then have two o's in a row? No, because need='o' means the next char should be o; the previous char was i (since pattern alternates and previous position even). Let's verify S="ii": after processing, target prefix "ioi" (positions i,o,i), previous char i, next need o. Append o -> "ioio". Good. S="oi": after processing, target prefix "ioi" (insert i, o, i), previous i, next o. Append o -> "ioio". Good.

But what about insertions between two S chars that are not immediately before the second S char? In our construction, all insertions are immediately before the S char that follows them (or at end). This is valid because arbitrary positions. Good.

Our construction would place insertions before b if needed. It doesn't matter; any placement is valid. The count is what matters.

```python
import sys

def main():
    S = sys.stdin.readline().strip()
    need = 'i'
    ans = 0
    for ch in S:
        if ch != need:
            ans += 1
        need = 'o' if ch == 'i' else 'i'
    if need == 'o':
        ans += 1
    print(ans)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

```python
import sys

def main():
    S = sys.stdin.readline().strip()
    need = 'i'
    ans = 0
    for ch in S:
        if ch != need:
            ans += 1
        need = 'o' if ch == 'i' else 'i'
    if need == 'o':
        ans += 1
    print(ans)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

```python
import sys

def main():
    S = sys.stdin.readline().strip()
    need = 'i'
    ans = 0
    for ch in S:
        if ch != need:
            ans += 1
        need = 'o' if ch == 'i' else 'i'
    if need == 'o':
        ans += 1
    print(ans)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

```python
import sys

def main():
    S = sys.stdin.readline().strip()
    need = 'i'
    ans = 0
    for ch in S:
        if ch != need:
            ans += 1
        need = 'o' if ch == 'i' else 'i'
    if need == 'o':
        ans += 1
    print(ans)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

```python
import sys

def main():
    S = sys.stdin.readline().strip()
    need = 'i'
    ans = 0
    for ch in S:
        if ch != need:
            ans += 1
        need = 'o' if ch == 'i' else 'i'
    if need == 'o':
        ans += 1
    print(ans)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

        if ch != need:
            ans += 1
        need = 'o' if ch == 'i' else 'i'
    if need == 'o':

    need = 'i'
    ans = 0
    for ch in S:
        if ch != need:
            ans += 1
        need = 'o' if ch == 'i' else 'i'
    if need == 'o':
        ans += 1
    print(ans)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

```python
import sys

def main():
    S = sys.stdin.readline().strip()
    need = 'i'
    ans = 0
    for ch in S:
        if ch != need:
            ans += 1
        need = 'o' if ch == 'i' else 'i'
    if need == 'o':
        ans += 1
    print(ans)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

```python
import sys

def main():
    S = sys.stdin.readline().strip()
    need = 'i'
    ans = 0
    for ch in S:
        if ch != need:
            ans += 1
        need = 'o' if ch == 'i' else 'i'
    if need == 'o':
        ans += 1
    print(ans)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

```python
import sys

def main():
    S = sys.stdin.readline().strip()
    need = 'i'
    ans = 0
    for ch in S:
        if ch != need:
            ans += 1
        need = 'o' if ch == 'i' else 'i'
    if need == 'o':
        ans += 1
    print(ans)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

import sys

```python
import sys

def main():
    S = sys.stdin.readline().strip()
    need = 'i'
    ans = 0
    for ch in S:
        if ch != need:
            ans += 1
        need = 'o' if ch == 'i' else 'i'
    if need == 'o':
        ans += 1
    print(ans)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

```python
import sys

def main():
    S = sys.stdin.readline().strip()
    need = 'i'
    ans = 0
    for ch in S:
        if ch != need:
            ans += 1
        need = 'o' if ch == 'i' else 'i'
    if need == 'o':
        ans += 1
    print(ans)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

No, because need='o' means the next char should be o; the previous char was i (since pattern alternates and previous position even). S="oi": after processing, target prefix "ioi" (insert i, o, i), previous i, next o. Append o -> "ioio". Good.

But what about insertions between two S chars that are not immediately before the second S char? In our construction, all insertions are immediately before the S char that follows them (or at end). This is valid because arbitrary positions. Good.

Our construction would place insertions before b if needed. It doesn't matter; any placement is valid. The count is what matters.

import sys

def main():
    S = sys.stdin.readline().strip()
    need = 'i'
    ans = 0
    for ch in S:
        if ch != need:
            ans += 1
        need = 'o' if ch == 'i' else 'i'
    if need == 'o':
        ans += 1
    print(ans)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Target positions: pos1 i (matched S1), pos2 o (inserted), pos3 i (matched S2), pos4 o (appended). T="ioio". Good. If need='o' after a match, e.g., S="ioi": pos1 i, pos2 o, pos3 i, append o. T="ioio". Good. So append always o.

Now, let's think if there is any scenario where after processing all S, need='o' but the last target position is odd and the appended char is o, but what if the final string would then have two o's in a row? No, because need='o' means the next char should be o; the previous char was i (since pattern alternates and previous position even). S="oi": after processing, target prefix "ioi" (insert i, o, i), previous i, next o. Append o -> "ioio". Good.

But what about insertions between two S chars that are not immediately before the second S char? In our construction, all insertions are immediately before the S char that follows them (or at end). This is valid because arbitrary positions. Good.

Our construction would place insertions before b if needed. It doesn't matter; any placement is valid. The count is what matters.

```python
import sys

def main():
    S = sys.stdin.readline().strip()
    need = 'i'
    ans = 0
    for ch in S:
        if ch != need:
            ans += 1
        need = 'o' if ch == 'i' else 'i'
    if need == 'o':
        ans += 1
    print(ans)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

Good. So append always o.

This is valid because arbitrary positions. Good.

```python
import sys

def main():
    S = sys.stdin.readline().strip()
    need = 'i'
    ans = 0
    for ch in S:
        if ch != need:
            ans += 1
        need = 'o' if ch == 'i' else 'i'
    if need == 'o':
        ans += 1
    print(ans)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

```python
import sys

def main():
    S = sys.stdin.readline().strip()
    need = 'i'
    ans = 0
    for ch in S:
        if ch != need:
            ans += 1
        need = 'o' if ch == 'i' else 'i'
    if need == 'o':
        ans += 1
    print(ans)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

```python
import sys

def main():
    S = sys.stdin.readline().strip()
    need = 'i'
    ans = 0
    for ch in S:
        if ch != need:
            ans += 1
        need = 'o' if ch == 'i' else 'i'
    if need == 'o':
        ans += 1
    print(ans)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

```python
import sys

def main():
    S = sys.stdin.readline().strip()
    need = 'i'
    ans = 0
    for ch in S:
        if ch != need:
            ans += 1
        need = 'o' if ch == 'i' else 'i'
    if need == 'o':
        ans += 1
    print(ans)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```
